The MAGA Movement Is Splintering Without Charlie Kirk
2026-09-10

The MAGA Movement Is Splintering Without Charlie Kirk

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. When Charlie Kirk was assassinated last September, many of his allies on the right memorialized him as a hero of free speech and the founder of an enduring political movement. Critics on the left tended to emphasize his long history of offensive remarks and dismiss him as little more than a troll. With a year of distance, neither view appears complete. Since his death, the MAGA movement has stumbled: Its factions are bickering, and Republicans are facing potentially disastrous midterm elections. President Trump seems disconnected from his base and reality. The current plight points to Kirk’s rare ability as a political node, someone who united otherwise disparate forces under one umbrella—and also to how much the movement depended on Kirk personally. Just how fragile a peace Kirk had managed to keep became clear three months after his killing, at an annual conference of Turning Point USA, the organization he’d co-founded. At the summit, feuding parties each tried to claim Kirk’s endorsement of their views. Ben Shapiro, the Daily Wire impresario, used the occasion to blast the broadcaster Tucker Carlson for conducting softball interviews with anti-Semites and other white supremacists. Citing Kirk’s stiff-arming of the Hitler-admiring influencer Nick Fuentes, whose followers were sometimes ejected from Turning Point events, Shapiro assailed Carlson for giving Fuentes a platform. Carlson mounted a counterattack in that evening’s keynote speech, arguing that it was Shapiro who was betraying Kirk’s commitment to open debate. These conflicts among leading right-wing figures have continued ever since. Kirk’s widow, Erika, now leads Turning Point and is well-regarded, but she does not seem to have the muscle that her husband did to quell conflicts. Carlson has become a furious critic of Trump and has vowed to create a rival political movement (although his ability to follow through is in question). Candace Owens, another prominent media personality, has become a proponent of conspiracy theories about Kirk’s death and has baselessly suggested at various times that the Israeli or French governments or “Egyptians” were involved. Fuentes has become a more influential commentator, but overall, MAGA media figures are seeing splintering audiences. No movement leader is as important as Trump, but the president has also had a tumultuous year. He is obsessed with building a ballroom at the White House—in part as a legacy project, but also perhaps because Kirk’s assassination and the attempts on his own life have instilled an understandable sense of paranoia that makes having a secure space at his residence feel imperative. As Trump has become more cloistered and withdrawn from public appearances, he has lost touch with his base, and he is pursuing an unpopular long-term war in Iran that betrays his campaign platform and writing off voters’ concerns about the economy and inflation. These dynamics, along with the natural pendulum swing against the sitting president’s party in midterm elections, mean that Republicans have bleak expectations for November. Not only are Democrats leading in polls of the generic House ballot and gaining in several Senate forecasts, but GOP voters are less enthusiastic than their Democratic counterparts. The problems are particularly acute among young voters. Although that group is typically heavily Democratic, Trump nearly matched Kamala Harris’s showing with the demographic in 2024, for which Turning Point received a great deal of credit. Today, however, young voters disapprove of Trump by wide margins—even greater than the general population. Young people who voted for Trump are also more likely to regret their vote than older ones, a recent Navigator poll found. Young men have turned against the president more than almost any other group has. What’s notable is that this drop in support and enthusiasm for Trump and Republicans comes at the same time that Turning Point is growing. As Reuters reported today, the organization has expanded since Kirk’s death, especially on campuses, but the people flocking to these chapters aren’t uniformly loyal to Trump. Turning Point could yet be an important presence in the midterms. The group was central to the Trump campaign’s plan for voter turnout in the 2024 election, helping deliver the president his first popular-vote victory among his three tries. Wired reports that Turning Point once again plans to spend large sums on field operations from now until the midterms. Kirk’s legacy on free speech and debate also remains up for grabs. As I wrote shortly after the assassination, an ironic phenomenon was emerging in which Kirk was being praised for his commitment to open discourse at the same time that right-wing figures were attempting to stifle any criticism of him. Some professors who were fired or punished in that period have since been reinstated or paid damages, but Trump and his allies have abandoned even a perfunctory commitment to free speech. Many of the tensions on display today have always been present in the MAGA movement. Trumpism is a big tent; it includes populists and plutocrats, isolationists and interventionists, and a dozen more dyads. The seams would inevitably have become visible at some point, but in retrospect, Kirk appears to have helped stave that off by maintaining connections across many factions and the sway to keep figures such as Fuentes outside. In his absence, the conflicts have come into the open. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: Lonnie Bunch, the secretary of the Smithsonian Institution, was already slipping into the past tense when we walked together through the National Museum of American History in February. Occasionally, as we stood before an exhibit, he’d discuss his role leading the largest museum system in the world as though he had already stepped away. Even then, there wasn’t a question of if he would retire soon, but how soon. He spoke longingly of a future life in which he took fewer flights, slept in fewer hotels, spent more time with his grandchildren. He desperately wanted more time to write. He told me he still has more books in him. Now he will get the time. Explore. The novelist Jackie Collins imagined women enjoying equality in the bedroom and the boardroom, Sophie Gilbert writes. Are we any closer to living in that world? Read. Emma Cline’s Switzy might not make readers happy, but it could strike them with moments of sublime clarity, Hillary Kelly argues. What Makes Tennis One of the Greatest Games
2026-09-10

What Makes Tennis One of the Greatest Games

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of Time-Travel Thursdays, a journey through The Atlantic’s archives to contextualize the present. Sign up here. The American tennis player Ben Shelton blasted a 146-mile-an-hour ace past the Spaniard Carlos Alcaraz at 3:33 a.m. yesterday. It was the final shot of a U.S. Open quarterfinal match that had begun 4 hours and 28 minutes before, during which the two men contested five sets, two tiebreakers, and many points so long and improbable that they made the television announcers giggle with delight. When it was done, Shelton and Alcaraz embraced warmly at the net, as if in mutual appreciation for having shared something so grand. The contest, which Shelton afterward described as a “war,” was an emphatic affirmation of a claim made by Arthur Stanwood Pier in The Atlantic 123 years ago: that tennis “is entitled to the place of supremacy among games.” If the case for this assertion is clear today, it was perhaps less obvious in 1903, when the question of which sport should hold such a title was no small matter among men—particularly elite white men—who spent a good deal of time discussing the import of athletics and manliness. Tennis, no matter how much Pier insisted, usually did not rate a mention at all. Far from being only amusements or diversions then, sports were considered serious undertakings that had the job of making men men and rescuing the nation from softness and degeneracy. These contests, the thinking went, offered a way—short of participating in actual warfare—to develop martial virtues, cultivate strength, and channel aggression. The value of a sport, Pier noted in his essay, was not measured so much “by the physical exercise it necessitates, as by the satisfaction and outlet it gives to the spirit of combat that troubles us.” By this measure, football—dangerous and deliberately martial in character—seemed to have been the most valuable game. Already firmly established at the turn of the century as a popular spectacle and staple of American collegiate life, the sport appeared to offer an ideal arena for masculinity, with its inherent risks and demand for coordinated action. “Every manly man must have a certain amount of courage, physical and mental,” the famed University of Michigan coach Fielding H. Yost wrote in 1905, “and no football player can long be without it.” The sport’s most sanguine supporters embraced the game’s violence as a precondition to cultivating manly men, even at the cost of serious injury or death. Two years after the 1894 Harvard-Yale game—the so-called Springfield Massacre that left four men seriously hurt and led to a temporary suspension of the rivalry—Senator Henry Cabot Lodge defended such contests in a speech at Harvard’s commencement. He minced no words, using the imperialist and anti-immigrant language underlying much athletic talk of the moment: “The time given to athletic contests and the injuries incurred on the playing field are part of the price which the English-speaking race has paid for being world conquerors.” Tennis—genteel, clubby, and historically played by women as well as men—seemed unlikely to do such work. As Robert D. Osborn, an early authority on the game, wrote in 1881, it was a sport of “exceeding delight,” particularly when played in what he described as its ideal conditions. “The scene should be laid in a well-kept garden lawn. There should be a bright warm sun overhead, and just sufficient breeze whispering through the trees and stirring the petals of the flowers to prevent the day from being sultry,” Osborn wrote. “Near at hand, under the cool shadow of a tree, there should be strawberries and cream.” This seemed a decidedly peaceful game—one for people who were taking a rest after conquering the world, as opposed to getting ready to do so. In making his case for tennis in The Atlantic, Pier confessed that the risks of the game were nothing like those of football. The inherent dangers were fewer, the injuries less “heroic.” But perhaps the calm of tennis was a good thing. Pier, a student at Harvard in the 1890s, was possibly in attendance for the speech in which Lodge justified the injuries that had occurred during football games. Without calling out the senator directly, Pier wondered whether such perils were necessary: What if the “injuries and deaths that sometimes take place in our rougher sports should not be viewed as glorifying these forms of contest” but instead as “deplorable calamities”? Tennis offered another way to make men of strength and vigor while allowing them to remain gentlemen with their bodies fully intact. Its ample physical demands—the game is “ardent, not phlegmatic,” Pier assured—were matched by an intellectual and psychological challenge. Pier tried to convey this complexity by way of lengthy descriptions of match play. Hemingway on bullfighting it was not, but something deeper emerged from the depiction. The purpose of the sport was to develop attributes of peace rather than war: A “steadfastness of aim and purpose, a better temper, and a kinder heart; here, through striving with your fellow man, you may learn to love him,” Pier wrote. This was a different kind of strength and vigor, one that included physical prowess but ultimately went beyond it. Dancing around the court the other night, Shelton and Alcaraz were playing a game that would’ve been barely recognizable to Pier in its speed and power. (Their attire would surely have made him blush.) But the subtle graces of the “lawn tennis” Pier wished to promote were still there, in the complex calculations and fluid coordination that clinched each point. Watch Shelton’s slide into a shot at the baseline, or the elegant arc of Coco Gauff’s swing as she defeated Mirra Andreeva in three sets during yesterday’s women’s quarterfinal. For all of the grand claims made about the purpose of sports, perhaps Pier’s tribute to tennis landed on the simplest point of all: “Is not this one of life’s priceless pleasures?”
The Atlantic
What Bush Understood That Trump Doesn’t
2026-09-10

What Bush Understood That Trump Doesn’t

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
The current New York City mayor, Zohran Mamdani, has affronted one of his predecessors, Rudy Giuliani, by taking the inflammatory step of deciding to attend the city’s 9/11 commemoration. “It offends me,” Giuliani proclaimed earlier this week on Newsmax. One might suspect that the nature of Mamdani’s offense lay in his being a politician who is associating himself with a day that Giuliani prefers to associate with Giuliani. But the former mayor proceeded to lay out a much darker rationale. “They’re taking over certain parts of America and certain parts of Europe. They have every desire to take us over. This is not ‘they want to be just another religion,’” he said of Muslims. “Mamdani is a clear, a clear supporter of that. All of the Muslims he supports are murderers!” Like many of Giuliani’s utterances, this one has no connection to reality. Yet his assertion that Mamdani harbors some secret desire to conquer the United States in the name of Islam, that his religion is the skeleton key to a web of sinister motivations, is now treated on the right as almost banal. A quarter century after 9/11, delirious anti-Muslim bigotry is almost routine in the political rhetoric of many conservatives. The conventional account of Donald Trump’s political career explains his rise to power as a populist revolt against Republican orthodoxy. The GOP elite had lost touch with their base by endorsing immigration reform, free trade, or forever wars in the Middle East, which created an opening that Trump exploited. Read: What the Islamophobic attacks on Mamdani reveal That story is largely true, but it misses an important issue. The President George W. Bush–era Republican Party after 9/11 insisted that the United States was at war not with a religion but with an idea. America opposed radical Islamism, not Islam. We stood for freedom as a universal human principle. Not all voters on the right accepted this formulation. Some believed that the United States was at war with Islam itself. By 2007, conservative Republicans viewed Islam negatively by a margin of more than 30 points; Democrats, independents, and moderate Republicans all held net-favorable views. Ten years later, nearly 80 percent of Republicans believed that the values of Islam were at odds with American values. One of the most important and ominous notions that Trump has seized upon and legitimized is that Muslims are inherently un-American. Anti-Muslim sentiment sprang up organically in the immediate aftermath of the 9/11 attacks, but Bush’s forceful defense of pluralism confined it to the rantings of Ann Coulter, Michael Savage, and other fringe reactionaries. After Coulter called on the United States in 2001 to “invade their countries, kill their leaders and convert them to Christianity,” National Review dropped her column. As Bush passed from the scene and was replaced by Barack Obama, some right-wing-outrage entrepreneurs recognized an opportunity to redefine the War on Terror as a war against Islam. Trump himself was among the first to see the opening. In 2010, a right-wing backlash arose in opposition to a proposal for a Muslim cultural center, modeled on the YMCA or a Jewish community center, in downtown New York. The most hysterical opponents depicted the center, which planned to call itself Park51, as a “victory mosque” celebrating the destruction of the World Trade Center. Other conservatives merely insisted that it was “insensitive” for Muslim Americans to open a center in the general neighborhood of Ground Zero. Trump inserted himself into the controversy, proposing that he purchase the property to halt construction on the putatively offensive site. In his offer letter (which, naturally, he made public), Trump called it a “very serious, inflammatory, and highly divisive situation that is destined, in my opinion, to only get worse.” David Letterman asked him, “Does this, in fact, suggest that we are officially at war with Muslims?” Trump, who was still in the formative stage of his transition from celebrity to right-wing rabble-rouser, replied, “Well, somebody knocked down the World Trade Center.” He later added, “Somebody’s blowing us up. Somebody’s blowing up buildings, and somebody’s doing lots of bad stuff.” Trump’s first campaign was pockmarked with undisguised bigotry against Muslims. He falsely claimed that Muslims in New Jersey had cheered the 9/11 attacks en masse, and he called for surveillance of mosques, a database of Muslims in America, and a ban on Muslims entering the United States. He associated the sitting president with Islam by incessantly using his middle name. He has continued to attack Muslims as disloyal or un-American. He falsely claimed that Representative Ilhan Omar of Minnesota, a Democrat, had praised al-Qaeda, and he demanded that she and Reprepresentative Rashida Tlaib of Michigan, both of whom are U.S. citizens, be sent “back from where they came.” He insinuated that Mamdani has no right to reside in the United States: “A lot of people are saying he’s here illegally.” The through line connecting these smears is the assumption that Muslims cannot be Americans, even if they arrive legally, even if they are born here. Trump has made this point explicitly and told Sean Hannity in 2016, “Assimilation has been very hard. It’s almost—I won’t say nonexistent, but it gets to be pretty close. And I’m talking about second- and third-generation. They come—they don’t—for some reason, there’s no real assimilation.” This sort of rhetoric has grown so common that episodes such as the uproar against Mamdani have come to feel like normal politics, rather than paranoia and rank discrimination. The New York Post alone has published at least eight stories (as of press time) hyping the Giuliani-fueled controversy. Some relatives of 9/11 victims have claimed that Mamdani’s presence at the observance would be a distraction (even though mayoral attendance has been a regular feature throughout its existence). Others have depicted their anger as a protest against Mamdani’s current agenda. “It’s not about his religion or where he came from. It’s about the policies he has today,” one explained. Is it the plan for free buses? The pied-à-terre tax? Asma Khalid: Islamophobia has corroded American values That Mamdani wishes to attend the ceremony and has denounced the 9/11 attacks as heinous terrorism ought to serve as satisfactory evidence that, whatever shortcomings he may have, he is not a supporter of al-Qaeda. Indeed, it might well be offensive if Mamdani did not want to attend the ceremony. The New York Post would need to publish phone-book-size editions to convey its full outrage. The logic of Islamophobia rejects out of hand that it is possible for Mamdani to oppose terrorism. 9/11 was, in this view, a battle in a war against Islam, so he is, by dint of his faith, the enemy. Thus, his desire to commemorate the tragedy, like Muslims’ desire to have a YMCA of their own in New York City, must be understood as revealing hidden malevolence. If you had asked Bush after 9/11 to imagine a future within his lifetime in which New York had a Muslim mayor who would join the city in mourning the attacks, he’d surely have considered it the ultimate vindication of the ideals he sought to foster. But Republicans have rejected Bush’s understanding of 9/11 and its meaning in favor of Trump’s. The events of the past decade, and especially the past six months, have clarified that the primary grounds for this repudiation does not seem to be Trump’s abhorrence for wars of choice in the Middle East. It is that Bush denied and suppressed contempt for Muslims, while Trump has honored and nurtured this hatred. *Image sources: Christy Bowe / Getty; Jim WATSON / AFP / Getty; Michael M. Santiago / Getty; Chip Somodevilla / Getty.

The Los Angeles Rams Are Trying to Defeat Time
2026-09-10

The Los Angeles Rams Are Trying to Defeat Time

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Usually, when the San Francisco 49ers and the Los Angeles Rams play, it’s a relatively short commute. But this evening, the teams will kick off the NFL’s first-ever regular-season game in Australia. The two teams have taken diametrically opposite approaches to preventing the 8,000-mile journey from disorienting their stars. The 49ers boarded their 16-hour flight to Melbourne nine days ago and have spent the intervening time training, watching Aussie rules football, and winning the majority of local support. The Rams landed yesterday, just 24 hours before the game. They’re betting on being in Australia so briefly that players’ brains and bodies won’t notice the difference. That makes the game the most high-profile test of jet-lag-evasion techniques in American sports history. Anyone who has hurled across several time zones in one go knows how wide-awake and helplessly sleepy you can feel at inappropriate hours upon landing—a major inconvenience for professional football players, whose career depends on how they perform during one very specific period. Circadian rhythms can also affect performance in less obvious ways. Compounds that encourage inflammation, for instance, spike at night, when the body is doing less and can direct more energy toward fighting infection. A new time zone throws that optimal function off tilt and can cause stomach issues, cognitive impairment, headaches, and mood changes. Teams at a circadian disadvantage tend to play worse. One study that analyzed every NFL game from 1970 to 2011 with a kickoff after 8 p.m. ET found that West Coast teams did better than oddsmakers expected in two out of every three games, regardless of whether they were home or away. Head coaches are also more likely to make the risky call to attempt an offensive play on a fourth down during evening games when they travel west compared with any other direction. Because the consequences of jet lag are well established, the real question is how to avoid them. In 2021, 26 sleep researchers and clinicians articulated their best answer in the journal Sports Medicine. Among other recommendations, they noted that fully recovering from jet lag takes one day for each time zone crossed. The 49ers seem to have followed that model by leaving more than a week ahead of the game. (Melbourne is 17 hours ahead of California, but for body-clock purposes, that’s the same as being seven hours behind.) Kyle Shanahan, the 49ers’ head coach, told reporters last month that he would have loved to have just shown up in Australia the day before but that the experts he’d spoken with had all recommended arriving earlier. “I was a little bit on the fence,” he said. “Science wasn’t.” The Rams, by contrast, are attempting to not switch their body clock at all. They have spent the past few weeks practicing in the late afternoon, which aligns with Friday’s mid-morning kickoff in Melbourne. Their head coach, Sean McVay, said at a Sunday press conference that the team will “have kind of a rhythm” when they land. “We’ve tested it out with some guys, and we feel like it’s been positive,” he added. The strategy is not without precedent: In 1994, Allen Johnson, an American hurdler, decided to live as if he were on East Coast time when competing in European tournaments. His coaching team strongly disagreed, but he won four world championships and Olympic gold. Last year, the Rams arrived in London the day before a game against a strong Jacksonville Jaguars team and won. Jamie Zeitzer, a circadian physiologist and a co-director of Stanford’s Center for Sleep and Circadian Sciences, described the Rams’ strategy to me as “high risk, high reward.” If the Rams really feel as though they’re playing in the afternoon, they might have a slight advantage over opponents who feel as though they’re playing in the morning. During the late afternoon, circadian rhythms prompt optimal muscle strength and coordination, and core body temperature peaks, increasing alertness with it. Plus, a week might not be long enough for the 49ers to stop suffering the effects of their travel. Some people just take longer to recover, and a long-haul flight, jet lag, hotel life, and unfamiliar foods can lead to days of poor sleep, Zeitzer said. The 49ers have spent their September experiencing time as a pliable, erratic force. Shanahan told reporters that he was “a little confused” in a recent press conference, during which he also struggled to identify the day of the week. (It was Monday.) Still, Zeitzer thinks the Rams have chosen the worse jet-lag strategy. If the players are to get any rest at all, their 24-hour stretch in Melbourne before game time needs to be carefully managed. From his experience consulting with professional teams, adults are hard to control. Meeta Singh, a doctor who has served as a sleep consultant for multiple NFL teams, thinks that players simply won’t be able to sleep. Singh is one of the co-authors of the Sports Medicine recommendations, which suggest that athletes maintain a “reduced exercise schedule” and do hand-eye-coordination drills in the 72 hours after a long-haul flight. The Rams won’t even be in Australia for 72 hours. Sleep became a major metric for all sorts of professional sports teams after the NBA distributed more than 2,000 Oura rings to its players during the depths of the coronavirus pandemic. “Once you can show a coach numbers, sleep stops being a soft topic and becomes something they can manage, like training load or nutrition,” Singh told me. Many NFL teams now have a sleep specialist and a performance-science team working on circumventing jet lag. To prep players for time shifts before they depart, 14 NFL teams, including the 49ers, have installed in their training facilities lighting systems designed to mold circadian rhythms. The system, called BrainLit, mimics daylight according to a “recipe” that each team specifies, Kyle Harris, the CEO of the company, told me. On the 49ers’ flight to Melbourne—on the world’s largest and most expensive passenger airliner, with lie-flat seats that extend to seven feet—each player received his own sleep guidelines. Tom Clark, a sports scientist who advises NFL teams with international games, told me that many teams establish each player’s chronotype, or natural preference to sleep and wake at certain hours, and use that information to prescribe optimal sleep habits. Roger Goodell, the commissioner of the NFL, eventually wants 16 international games to take place each season. By the time Super Bowl LXI rolls around this February, teams will have played in Rio de Janeiro, London, Paris, Madrid, Munich, and Mexico City. For future seasons, the league is considering adding games in the United Arab Emirates and Japan; Goodell insists that an NFL team will be based outside the U.S. in his lifetime. Perhaps such a punishing schedule will settle debates once and for all about how to handle jet lag. Whatever the marginal impact of sleep strategy, though (and some games are decided on the margins), usually “the better team will win even if they’re playing on the moon,” Clark said. And the Rams are this year’s Super Bowl favorites.
The Atlantic
What Colin Powell Did on 9/11
2026-09-10

What Colin Powell Did on 9/11

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Secretary of State Colin Powell was having breakfast with Peru’s president in Lima’s historic center on September 11, 2001, when an aide passed him a note to inform him that a plane had struck the World Trade Center. Minutes later, he received a second note. “Oh my God,” Powell said aloud. Later that morning, as the scale of the destruction in New York and Washington was starting to become clear, Powell delivered remarks in a cavernous hotel ballroom. Fears of additional attacks had forced the closure of U.S. airspace. Even senior Cabinet members remained in the dark about President George W. Bush’s whereabouts. Peru’s leaders knew that the secretary of state—the fourth in line to succeed the president—would likely need to depart. But Powell, a former chairman of the Joint Chiefs before he was picked by Bush to lead the Foreign Service, opted to stay—at least for a while. The former general was in Peru, along with foreign ministers from across the Americas, to cement a new hemisphere-wide democracy charter that would enshrine the region’s commitment to free and fair elections and the rule of law. I was there as a Lima-based correspondent covering the visit. Countries across Latin America were emerging from a long period of despotism and military dictatorship. The United States wanted to make sure the region didn’t backslide. “A terrible, terrible tragedy has befallen my nation, but it has befallen all of the nations of this region, all the nations of the world, and befallen all those who believe in democracy,” Powell told the room full of officials, his voice grave but steady. “It is important that I remain here for a bit longer in order to be part of the consensus of this new charter.” As the magnitude of the events in the United States washed over me, Powell’s composure and his focus on a point of principle stood out. Even at an extreme, wounded moment in America’s history, he was affirming that the country’s security was inextricably bound up with how U.S. allies represented—or didn’t represent—the will of their people. Strong democracies would support America’s ability to keep itself safe. Powell could have bolted home. But he lingered to make the point. The ministers adopted the charter by acclamation, and by 12:30 p.m. eastern time, Powell had taken his leave for Washington, D.C.—and for everything that followed from the events of that day. This week, 25 years later, I watched a different secretary of state visit Lima. Marco Rubio is meeting Peruvian leaders today on a tour of Andean nations that have taken a turn to the political right. Given the magnitude of what followed 9/11—the wars, the excesses, the lives lost—Powell’s connection to Peru that day has receded from our collective memory. But his insistence on linking democracy promotion to national security seems remote. Donald Trump rose to power, in part, on the backlash to the post-9/11 wars in Iraq and Afghanistan. In his view, the idea of spreading democracy around the world was an expensive folly, and he’s sold his supporters on a very different approach to foreign policy. In Latin America, the Trump administration has pushed the “Donroe Doctrine,” a 21st-century version of the 19th-century Monroe Doctrine that’s designed to secure “American preeminence” and allied support for the president’s priorities of shutting out drugs, migrants, and U.S. rivals such as China. Trump’s second-term National Security Strategy elevates “flexible realism” and brass-tacks interests such as access to strategic resources in the region. “Democracy” is mentioned three times, while “interests” are cited 36 times. Mutual benefits with other countries can be pursued “without imposing on them democratic or other social change that differs widely from their traditions and histories,” the document says. In practice, that has meant partnerships with those willing to embrace Trump’s militarized approach to combatting drugs, crime, and illegal migration, regardless of those countries’ governing systems. The deportation of migrants to a nightmarish prison in El Salvador is a prime example, as is the ouster of the leftist Nicolás Maduro in Venezuela and the norm-busting oil deal made with Maduro’s deputy. Or consider the militarized counter-drug operations the Pentagon has pursued with Ecuadorean President Daniel Noboa, who at the same time has dissolved opposition parties and targeted rivals. Trump has enthusiastically waded into the region’s elections to endorse candidates who share his political vision: Javier Milei of Argentina, Abelardo de la Espriella of Colombia, Tito Asfura of Honduras. Meanwhile, the Trump administration’s campaign of boat strikes against alleged narco-traffickers in the Caribbean and the Pacific, which critics label as extrajudicial killings, grinds on. “Politeness and a good bit of decorum are all to the good, but over the last generation, things have gone too far,” Elbridge Colby, the Pentagon’s policy chief, told Latin American defense officials in a speech in Peru earlier this year. “Our rhetoric became wildly detached from the reality of our interests. We talked about the fate of people halfway around the world as if they were as important to us as our neighbors.” Instead of trying to build ideal societies in far-flung countries, Colby said, the United States would now advance its hard-nosed interests in its immediate neighborhood, using the U.S. military to help allies assert control over lawless areas, treating drug traffickers like the Pentagon treats jihadist groups, and blocking U.S. adversaries’ access to minerals and other key resources. What he dubbed the “Peace Corps” approach to reducing the throngs of migrants and crime headed toward the U.S. had failed, Colby said. If it’s a Latin American democracy that partners with Washington to achieve those goals, great. If it’s not, no one’s going to spend much time worrying about that. The State Department spokesperson Tommy Piggott told me in a written statement that the United States is “working together with our allies and partners to address the shared opportunities in our hemisphere, and to confront the shared challenges, including defeating the scourge of narcoterrorism that has killed so many.” Many Latin Americans are ready to at least entertain this shift, according to polls. Spiraling crime, ineffective governance, and the hangover of the COVID era have made trading political liberties for security and economic growth a conceivable option. One of the most popular politicians in the region is Nayib Bukele, the Salvadoran strongman responsible for some of the region’s most flagrant democratic reversals but whose taming of rampant gang violence has made him an icon to many. Democratic backsliding has also occurred on Latin America’s left. After this year’s Colombian elections, Gustavo Petro, de la Espriella’s leftist predecessor, made unsubstantiated allegations of fraud intended to sow doubt about the results. Rubio presents a plausible case that the armed groups now fueling rampant violence across many Latin American nations are the public’s paramount concern. Those groups not only traffic drugs; they are in some places better bankrolled and armed than the state. In stops in Colombia and Ecuador this week, Rubio made an impassioned case for action. “You have to make a choice here. We’re going to choose on the side of leaders that are going to be serious about taking these groups on and eliminating them once and for all,” he said after talks with Noboa yesterday. “Otherwise, those groups—those terrorist groups—will eat these countries up.” But the people of the region are also setting limits on how far America’s reach is allowed to extend. Noboa has allowed U.S. forces to begin joint military operations, but Ecuadoreans rejected a proposal last year that would have permitted the United States to establish military bases in the country. This week, Rubio announced that Peru, under conservative President Keiko Fujimori, will be joining the administration’s “Shield of the Americas,” Trump’s militarized counter-drug program. It’s unclear how far Fujimori, whose father, Alberto Fujimori, is both lionized and vilified in Peru for his autocratic campaign against armed leftist movements in the 1990s, will feel she can go in cooperation with Washington. Read: Venezuela seems to be going … well? “What I see is less of a story about Trump and his government urging allies to go down this road, and more a story of these sovereign countries, these societies, having this debate about the trade-offs between political liberties and anti-crime policy,” Brian Winter, a Latin America expert who is the editor in chief of Americas Quarterly, told me. Based on my years of working in Latin America, the long shadow of yanqui meddling is hard to overstate. And polls show that many people in Latin America today see China, not the United States, as the more reliable superpower. The U.S. interest in promoting democracy as a pillar of national security didn’t begin in 2001. It may have reached its peak in the 1990s. But the grand U.S.-led experiments of Afghanistan and Iraq, as well as movements elsewhere to overthrow dictators, dominated the first decade of this century—with disappointing results. Several years after 9/11, I was in Baghdad, chronicling the U.S. effort to extract Iraq from the sectarian bloodshed unleashed by the 2003 invasion. Since then, Iraq has managed to emerge as a deeply flawed but surprisingly enduring democracy. I covered Libyan rebels’ overthrow of the dictator Muammar Qaddafi in Tripoli in 2011. Libya remains a failed state, 15 years later. And I was covering the Biden administration when the two-decade effort to build a stable democracy in Afghanistan fell apart in a matter of days. Perhaps not surprisingly, the consensus of many Americans, and certainly of MAGA supporters, is that the push to elevate democracy in the farthest reaches of the world proved to be a waste of time and money. That conclusion underpins the Trump administration’s approach in Latin America and beyond. When Trump went to war with Iran in February, democracy was an afterthought, and opponents to the country’s authoritarian regime were left to fend for themselves. But the failures of America’s democracy-promotion experiments have eclipsed notable successes across Eastern Europe and parts of Asia. Steven Levitsky, the director of the David Rockefeller Center for Latin American Studies at Harvard University, told me that Latin America has proven to be a democratic success story. The chief exceptions—among them Nicaragua, El Salvador, and Cuba—need to be set against the region’s largest nations. That big countries such as Brazil and Argentina have generally stayed the democratic course despite extreme inequality, high crime, and a global democratic recession, Levitsky said, “is a sign of extraordinary resilience.” Venezuela presents the most explicit test of how traditional U.S. values will play into Trump’s pursuit of what he sees as America’s national interest. Predictions that the country would descend into chaos after Maduro’s ouster have so far proved wrong. Yet the most tangible sign of American involvement in Venezuela’s affairs to date has been a new oil deal, which gives an office in the Pentagon a stake in the nation’s future output. Rubio has laid out a three-stage plan for shepherding Venezuela to legitimate and elected governance, and he has stressed that the country first needs to rebuild its legal and institutional structures. He argues that the oil pact will benefit Venezuelans. No date for elections—and therefore no date for democracy to return to Caracas—has been set. Powell, who died in 2021, might have had something to say about that. In the hotel ballroom in Lima, with the U.S. homeland under its most serious attack since Pearl Harbor, he talked about a different model, one that linked democracy to achieving prosperity and peace. He noted that the countries represented around him had their own tragic histories with terrorism—Peru’s battle with the Shining Path, for instance. “It is something we must all unite behind,” Powell said. “We unite behind it as democratic nations committed to individual liberties, committed to the rights of people to live in peace and freedom in a way in which they, and not terrorists, select their leaders or define how they will be governed.” It was the means, not only the ends, that he clung to that day.

Apple's own privacy paper describes Siri Recap transcribing speech on the iPhone, condensing that transcript, and sending the condensed text to Private Cloud Compute. The final output is a summary. That distinction matters, but it hardly makes the conversation disappear. Deleting the waveform does not delete the information extracted from it. Live Rewind works on the preceding 15 seconds of buffered audio and sounds a chime when activated. A chime cannot travel backward in time to ask permission. Siri Recap provides no audible signal at all, with Apple pointing to the absence of retained raw audio as the justification. Apparently, whether you deserve a heads-up depends on the output format. The article should also dial back the legal certainty. Consent laws have different scopes and exceptions; "11 states" is not a substitute for examining the applicable law and circumstances. Democrats Have Several Ways to Take Control of the Senate. Let’s Break Down Their Best Chance.
2026-09-10

Democrats Have Several Ways to Take Control of the Senate. Let’s Break Down Their Best Chance.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Welcome to Part 2 of our explainer about which party will control Congress after November’s midterm elections. As we wrote earlier this week, Democrats are a virtual lock to flip the House, but the Senate is another story. Despite Donald Trump’s unpopularity, most election observers put the odds of a Dem majority somewhere around a coin toss. Right now, the Senate has 53 Republicans vs. 47 members of the Democratic caucus. Given that Vice President J.D. Vance is the tiebreaker, Democrats need to pick up a net of at least four* seats in the midterms (more on that asterisk below). Defense might be the wrong word in Georgia, where polls show freshman Sen. Jon Ossoff handily beating a Republican challenger who keeps some questionable company. But in the states where incumbent Democrats are retiring, the party probably has to replace all of them to win a majority. Dems are on pretty safe ground in Minnesota, a blue state that bore the brunt of Trump’s immigration crackdown. In blue-leaning New Hampshire, though, some polls show a tight race between Democratic Rep. Chris Pappas and John Sununu, a former senator whose family is a GOP political dynasty there. The road looks rockiest in the swing state of Michigan. Abdul El-Sayed, a progressive, faces former Rep. Mike Rogers, whom my colleague Jim Newell describes as “a buttoned-up, country-club Republican from the George W. Bush era.” Rogers only narrowly lost a Senate race there in 2024, so Dems have work to do. The party’s best opportunities to pick up seats currently held by Republicans are probably North Carolina, Ohio, and Maine, but each has caveats. Polls show former North Carolina Gov. Roy Cooper spanking his MAGA-aligned opponent, but the state hasn’t sent a Democrat to the Senate since 2008. In Ohio, former Sen. Sherrod Brown is mounting a comeback bid in a onetime swing state that has gone red in the Trump era. And in Maine, Republican Sen. Susan Collins is vying for a sixth term against Troy Jackson, a former logger who replaced the scandal-ridden Graham Platner in an eleventh-hour switcheroo. But Collins has a history of overperforming, and polls suggest a close race. Even if Dems win all those races, though, they’d gain only three seats. Where’s the fourth? Nice counting. The difficulty of getting that majority-making seat helps show why Dems’ path is so tricky this year. One possible pickup is Texas (!). The state is conservative, but corruption allegations and a messy divorce threaten to sink state Attorney General Ken Paxton. He trails in most polls to Democrat James Talarico, a charismatic state lawmaker who Democrats are hoping can both turn out the party faithful and win over Republicans who can’t stomach Paxton. Elsewhere, former Rep. Mary Peltola of Alaska, who lost in 2024, hopes to return to Congress as a senator, and polling suggests she has a chance of beating Republican Dan Sullivan. And in Iowa, a state representative and former Paralympian named Josh Turek is angling for an upset win against GOP Rep. Ashley Hinson. (The polling is all over the place in that race.) Texas, Alaska, and Iowa all backed Trump three times, so they’re heavy lifts for Democrats. But stranger things have happened in wave elections, which November could become. Dems winning the Senate would all but end Trump’s legislative agenda. It would also make it harder to get conservative federal judges confirmed—including to the Supreme Court. It’s a big one. John Fetterman, Pennsylvania’s 6-foot-8 senator, seems to be wavering over which party he wants to belong to. Elected in 2022 as a progressive, Fetterman has increasingly voted with Republicans and criticized his party’s left flank. On Wednesday, he appeared by video at Trump’s midterm convention and pledged to work with the president to help Pennsylvania. Fetterman has said he’ll leave the Democrats if they become “the anti-Israel party,” stoking speculation that he could soon switch sides. In that case, Dems might have to net five seats to guarantee Senate control. Fetterman claims he won’t put his party through that. “Should the Senate flip in November, I will still be the 51st vote that I ran on,” he said yesterday. But Fetterman has a recent history of erratic behavior, and it’s not clear Democrats should trust his word. Which means, as tough as the party’s path looks right now, things could always get worse. Which Senate race this year most interests you and why? Let me know at theslatest@slate.com. Election Day is still almost two months away, so let’s transition to the immediate term: how to enjoy your evening. If you’re looking for a comfort watch: Call My Agent!, a delicious comedy series about a French talent agency, is back after several years off the air—and this time, it’s a movie. According to Slate’s Dana Stevens, you’ll enjoy the film even if you haven’t seen the show. But if you do binge the series first, watching it will be like “getting 24 consecutive deliveries of perfectly baked Parisian pastry.” Bon appétit! If you want to get metaphysical: College football is rife with scandals, but the latest one is particularly surreal. Alex Kirshner explains why Michigan’s latest controversy has spectators contemplating the nature of time itself. If you need some enrichment: What’s a “short name for a big explosive” (three letters)? What’s a “gamer cheer sometimes spelled with zeros” (four letters)? Give your brain a workout with today’s crossword. If you’re trying to understand what’s happening online: Raising a kid today requires spending a lot of time navigating digital platforms. There are WhatsApp chats, special apps, YouTube channels, subreddits, Facebook groups, Instagram reels … the chatter, advice, and drama can feel all-consuming. What Next: TBD host Lizzie O’Leary joins forces with ICYMI host Kate Lindsay to make sense of the Wild West that is the parenting internet. OK, time for me to leave you to your own devices. This Trump Speech Sent a Dangerous Message to Iran—and the Rest of the World
2026-09-10

This Trump Speech Sent a Dangerous Message to Iran—and the Rest of the World

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Sometimes I wonder whether President Trump simply doesn’t think before he speaks or whether he has a weird compulsion to blow up the world. It happened again Wednesday during his harangue at the Republican Midterm Convention, which was really a fancy name for a Trump campaign rally. Amid the usual rants of self-puffery and other fictions, he said this: If [Iran] had a nuclear weapon, I’d be calling the Supreme Leader and I’d be saying, ‘Mr. Supreme Leader, how are you, sir? Is there anything we can do for you?’—as opposed to bombing the crap out of him. Many in the audience laughed and applauded, when they should have gasped or moaned. Trump added, “We cannot let them have a nuclear weapon, it’s very simple”—as if that were the lesson of his little tale. But the lesson that any world leader—friend, foe, or in between—would draw from this speech is simpler still: I need to get some nuclear weapons as quickly as possible. Trump is telling the world’s leaders that he might bomb the crap out of them at any moment, or treat them like crap as a whim—but he would kiss the hems of their garments, bestow them with whatever favors they request, if they got themselves some nukes. Maybe this is why he treats Russia’s Vladimir Putin, China’s Xi Jinping, and North Korea’s Kim Jong-un with such respect. Maybe it’s not just that he envies their absolute power on the home front or their unabashedly lavish palaces. Maybe it’s also because, like him, they have enough nuclear bombs to destroy much of life on earth—and he fears they might do so unless he showers them with praise and honor. If this is part of his reasoning, he’s making two mistakes. First, he assumes that they’re as gullible as he is—that, since he believes the compliments they shovel his way, they’ll believe his shovelings too. This almost certainly is not the case; they are much more hardheaded and cynical in their views of the world and their interests. Second, he apparently doesn’t consider what anyone outside the realm of his political base might think. He probably would treat Iran’s leaders differently—certainly he wouldn’t bombard their country with no good reason—if they had a pocketful of nukes to fire back at him. That’s what “nuclear deterrence” is all about; the possession of nuclear weapons deters others from behaving aggressively. But a leader—especially the president of the United States—should know better than to say such things out loud. Doing so flashes a message in bright neon: You want to block me from bombing your country? You want me to be nice? Then go get yourself some nuclear weapons. To the extent Iran still has the technology and the chops to build some A-bombs, you can bet that its scientists are revving up the centrifuges to further enrich their remaining stocks of uranium—while they still can. Kim Jong-un long ago passed this hurdle—which, many have already concluded, is why Iran gets bombed while North Korea’s far more monstrous regime is tolerated, even spoken of respectfully. But to the extent Kim responds to other leaders’ signals, Trump’s comment may move him to accelerate the expansion of Pyongyang’s arsenal. Meanwhile, many of our allies—notably South Korea, Japan, and a few countries in Europe—will no doubt absorb the same message. They have already been thinking about abandoning their restraint when it comes to going nuclear. They have the technical capacity to make the leap, they live in an increasingly dangerous world, they no longer trust the United States to come to their aid in a crisis. And now Trump is loudly confirming the correlation they’ve considered but would rather not think too hard about, until now—that a nuclear arsenal can win them safety and honor from even the most disreputable foes. And when one country goes nuclear, the dominoes start to fall. If Iran builds nukes, Saudi Arabia would certainly follow suit. The royal family has said as much. If South Korea or Japan develops their own deterrent, China will accelerate their own deployments in order to overwhelm their deterrent. Regional arms races worldwide will ensue. There is a school of thought—an extreme branch of “International Realism”—that views nuclear proliferation as a peace-building process: the more nations that have nuclear bombs, the less likely their adversaries will start war. Maybe. But maybe not, even stipulating their premise that all leaders (including savage and adventurous ones) act rationally. New and overly eager nuclear powers might not take the time and care to erect the guardrails that the larger powers gradually installed over the decades—the spy satellites that gauge the size of an opponent’s arsenal, the early-warning sensors that detect an incoming attack, the command-control machinery that ensures only those authorized to push the button have the ability to do so. Even the superpowers’ guardrails—built and maintained at great expense during the Cold War—have failed at times, the radar mistaking flocks of geese for an ICBM attack or a computer mistaking a prerehearsed simulation for the real thing. At least the U.S. and Russia, oceans apart from each other, had half an hour to sort out the confusion before the missiles would land if they had been real. Countries much closer to each other might not have the time. In the real world, as opposed to in academic theory, a world bubbling over with nuclear weapons from many stovetops is more dangerous—not less. Meanwhile, almost every time Trump talks, he sets off another problem, at times to the point of lighting a book of matches or pouring gasoline on a looming fire, often without seeing what he’s doing, without considering that words have consequences. We are learning, more and more, that this is what happens when someone of Trump’s character becomes president. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Can Trump Buy Back Americans’ Love at $5,000 a Piece? Guess We’ll Find Out.
2026-09-10

Can Trump Buy Back Americans’ Love at $5,000 a Piece? Guess We’ll Find Out.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The first Republican midterm convention had all the simulacra of a real political convention. There were seats assigned by state and indicated by tall signs on poles, there was a host of precisely timed and deeply forgettable speeches by elected officials, and, of course, there was a keynote speaker. But those were all window dressing to hide what it really was: just another Trump rally. There was news—or at least an eye-popping statement designed to make headlines: Donald Trump promised that, so long as Republicans retained control of both the House and Senate, every American would receive a $5,000 check from the U.S. government. It was a statement perfectly calibrated the next day in newspapers and local news broadcasts. That does not, however, mean it’s likely to happen. Republicans are a long shot to keep the House and far from a lock to hold on to the Senate. There are obvious policy issues with this, as it would cost over a trillion dollars. There are obvious political issues with it: Trump has had issues trying to rally fiscally conservative Republicans behind far less ambitious agenda items. It also was perfectly hedged as a proposal, conditioned only on the scenario that the GOP manages to defy political gravity in November. Still, it’s something new, at a time when Trump’s “Lake America”–style gimmicks have gone stale—a replacement to standard promises from Trump’s first term about building a wall on the U.S.–Mexico border, ending foreign wars, and having so much winning that “your head will spin.” For those more likely to hear about politics from eight-second TikTok clips than 800-word news articles, Trump made masturbation jokes. The speakers preceding him were disjointed and all making their own pitches for why Democrats were bad and Republicans were good. Some touted the merits of last year’s Republican spending bill, now on its third or fourth name ahead of the midterms. Others focused on the evils of Democrats with the remarks of far-left streamer Hasan Piker, who has become a surrogate for a number of Democratic candidates, receiving particular attention. Some were candidates themselves, delivering versions of their own stump speech, just like the array of speakers who proceed Trump at every rally as a way to tide over the restless crowd before the main event. (The most noteworthy speaker before Trump was a recorded video from John Fetterman, who introduced his Republican colleague Dave McCormick. The iconoclastic Pennsylvanian did not back Trump in remarks that seemed designed more to influence Sunday-show bookers than Keystone State swing voters.) And just as there are occasional celebrity cameos at a Trump rally, there was one at the midterm convention, where early-aughts comic Adam Carolla did a routine. When Trump finally took the stage, he spoke for nearly two hours, just like a rally. In the front of the crowd were many of the usual Trump superfans who flock to every rally in the same way that fans of the Grateful Dead used to follow the rock group across the country. The president even shouted them out as he spoke—“the Front-Row Joes” and, of course, “Brick Suit,” a MAGA devotee who wears a suit with a brick pattern on it to show his ardent support for building a border wall with Mexico. The speech itself often felt like a rally speech. It was filled with digressions (Trump took shots at targets ranging from a New York Times writer to Michael Dukakis), and often the president spent an extended stretch as a fainthearted surrogate, urging voters to support Texas Senate nominee Ken Paxton, despite the fact that Paxton dresses badly. But, of course, a mega Trump rally is what the GOP needs right now. Part of the modern role of political conventions is to fix the fractures that emerge within a political party during a presidential primary and signify that everyone is united in a common task. In Trump’s GOP, that’s not an issue. While a handful of moderate dissidents are tacitly tolerated as a matter of expedience, most other heretics have been driven from the tent. The party is united around Trump; the challenge is whether a significant portion of Republicans will vote for anyone besides Trump. The traditional utility of Trump rallies in competitive elections is to energize the MAGA base. At a time when Republican enthusiasm is lagging, that’s what this was about. As Trump told viewers that his wish was for voters to pretend that his name was on the ballot, this was about trying to energize his diehard loyalists—the people who were ready to storm the Capitol on Jan. 6 but didn’t bother to show up in the voting booth in 2022—to turn out in November. Trump rallies have long been effective at this in specific races—Trump’s recent appearance in Myrtle Beach before a primary election in South Carolina may have been enough to steer that race to his preferred candidate—but this was just one big nationalized Trump rally. It might help some, but for anyone hoping it’ll swing the midterms, it probably wouldn’t be prudent to bet an entire $5,000 check. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The IRS Gave ICE Some Information It Really Shouldn’t Have. There Will Be Consequences for All of Us.
2026-09-10

The IRS Gave ICE Some Information It Really Shouldn’t Have. There Will Be Consequences for All of Us.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. The IRS arguably holds Americans’ most sensitive information, more so than any other federal agency, and for the better part of 2025, it quietly handed over that information, to the tune of about 47,000 records, to the Department of Homeland Security. This was done to aid in President Donald Trump’s deportation agenda, but that doesn’t mean it was legal: An appeals court this week found that the IRS violated federal law when it turned over the addresses of folks it deemed to lack permanent legal status. Now the agency and the employees who participated in the illicit information sharing could face civil and criminal consequences, including lawsuits from people who were deported after the IRS delivered their information to DHS. The IRS is just one agency that got roped into cooperating with the Trump administration amid its immigration crackdown last year. Under normal circumstances, the IRS is forbidden from disclosing Americans’ tax information to anyone, including to other federal agencies and even within the executive branch, something Congress codified into federal law in the aftermath of Watergate. However, there are a few narrow exceptions, including active non-tax-related criminal investigations. This is where Trump’s DHS began scheming, crafting a memorandum of understanding that took advantage of a loophole in a federal statute. The memorandum stated that the IRS could provide undocumented taxpayers’ addresses because a person is technically subject to criminal investigation if they remain in the U.S. beyond 90 days after receiving a final order of removal. The MOU was so controversial it drove former Acting IRS Commissioner Melanie Krause, whom Trump appointed in early 2025, to resign in protest last April, alongside other top IRS leadership. Nevertheless, (now) former Immigration and Customs Enforcement Director Todd Lyons wasted no time after the IRS deal was struck, requesting the last known addresses of 1.28 million undocumented individuals late that June. By July 1, the IRS was giving its employees the green light to process Lyons’ requests. However, according to the U.S. Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, per federal law, that blanket request shouldn’t have been honored. Instead, ICE should have provided the IRS with specific information about its criminal investigations before the IRS shared any of its records, including the addresses DHS had on file for the specified individuals; however, that did not happen for more than 90 percent of DHS’s records requests. As the appeals court concluded, the IRS “never confirmed whether ICE request contained information plausibly reflecting ‘the name and address of the taxpayer.’ ” In fact, the judges suggested, ICE could have submitted requests with addresses like “ ‘Don’t Care 12345,’ or ‘00000,’ and still received a taxpayer’s address.” That’s not all. If it had followed the law, ICE would have identified a point of contact at the agency for each taxpayer’s criminal investigation. Instead, the court found, Lyons was listed for all 1.28 million records requests—which is “facially impossible,” the judges wrote. The agency also failed to provide a reason why a taxpayer’s address would be relevant to its investigation. The bottom line is that the IRS dramatically moved away from its long-standing policy of closely guarding taxpayers’ sensitive information and reviewing records requests on a case-by-case basis to “a mass, automated review of millions of records at the press of a button.” What happens now? For its actions, the government could “face steep civil and criminal consequences for willful disclosure of information,” in violation of federal tax law—but it’s largely up to Congress to decide if and how to exact those consequences. The House Ways and Means Committee or the Senate Finance Committee could hold oversight hearings to dig into what occurred here and chose to penalize the IRS by pulling its funding or imposing guardrails on its work. The appetite to do so likely depends largely on whether Democrats retake the House in the upcoming midterms. Even more significantly, everyone who was swept up in DHS’s probe of addresses and was deported or subjected to ICE because the IRS handed over their tax records now has standing to sue. It’s not clear how many individuals were deported directly from the two agencies’ MOU, but we do know that arrests and deportations across the country have skyrocketed. However, those who were directly affected and are no longer on U.S. soil likely face an uphill battle to pursue legal action against the Trump administration. There’s another way this could all come back to bite the IRS. The agency has for decades carefully built up trust within America’s immigrant population by pushing them to file their taxes, no matter their status, and promising that this will not trigger deportation. With a new tax season beginning on Jan. 1, “some people will be wary of filing their tax returns,” Martin Sheil, a former IRS investigator and supervisory special agent for the agency’s Criminal Investigation Division, told me. “From an IRS perspective, it is their mission to ensure and promote voluntary compliance with the Internal Revenue Code.” The whole situation underscores the IRS’s politically fraught mission, which for decades, Sheil said, heavily discouraged its employees from making any illegal disclosures—even inadvertent ones—because people need to feel confident that their tax records are being protected. “Then you get into a wrestling match between agencies and mission priorities,” Sheil said. “ICE feels like their No. 1 one priority, laid out by the president, is to scoop up illegal immigrants, and they want to use any means necessary to assist them in meeting their mission priority.” But all federal agencies are at risk if Americans suddenly stop complying with the IRS—a trend that should majorly worry Congress. Maintaining the confidence of taxpayers is integral to keeping the federal government’s lights on, particularly when you consider that undocumented workers pay an estimated $90 billion in federal, state, and local taxes, despite being ineligible for most social programs, including Social Security and Medicare. Even a 1-percentage-point loss among the overall U.S. population would slash federal revenue by $40 billion. Over the past year, everyone in the U.S., regardless of legal status, has personally witnessed or read about ICE’s heavy-handed tactics across the country and is now learning about the unlawful relationship the IRS struck up with DHS. That could cumulatively inhibit people from complying with tax law, and this ought to alarm Congress, the body that has not just the authority to step in and investigate how the IRS and DHS have been working together, but an obligation to do so. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction. If you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! The episode also delves into how the court’s conservative majority uses doctrinal head-fakes to ensure that the president always wins in court. In the Amicus bonus episode, Madiba K. Dennie, of Balls and Strikes, joins Dahlia to unpack the justices’ attempts to prevent their papers from going public and analyzes the broader message that sends about SCOTUS. House GOP Leader Mike Johnson failed to pass an amendment that would cap the number of Supreme Court justices at nine. Friend of Slate Alexis Romero, an economic and environmental justice attorney, argues that this cynical move demonstrates that “Republicans are so scared of the electorate voting for judicial reform” that they are willing to try to sign away their own authority on the matter. Friend of Slate Steven Lubet, the Williams Memorial Professor Emeritus at Northwestern University Pritzker School of Law, explains how USPS was similarly weaponized after a postmaster refused to mail pamphlets highlighting the human-rights abuses perpetuated by slavery. The trial of Lindsay Clancy, the high-profile murder case that consumed the nation for weeks, ended in a mistrial last Friday. The jury was held up by a single juror, who refused to join his 11 fellow jurors in finding Clancy not guilty. I spoke with a trial attorney to understand how common a hung jury really is, why the judge in this case decided not to throw out the one unruly juror, and what the prosecution should do next. Trump has continuously violated the rights and interests of individual states for his personal and partisan political interests, an impeachable offense, argues friend of Slate Frank Bowman, a law professor and a former federal and state prosecutor. The president has specifically gone after blue states, following an unprecedented pattern that amounts to “an abuse of presidential power sufficiently egregious and disruptive of constitutional order to merit impeachment,” Bowman writes. On Call: Boss wrongly claimed IT deleted his files, refused to learn the one-click fix

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
There's no place like the Home directory, but the Documents folder is sometimes nicer ON CALL Friday morning is The Register's home for tech support stories, which we showcase in On Call – the weekly column made possible by readers generously sharing their experience of what it takes to fix things for the furious, fatuous, or feebleminded users that infest every workplace. This week, meet a reader we'll Regomize as "Lucia," who was once ordered to report immediately to a company director's office. "All of my files have gone," the director fumed, before insisting this was all the fault of the IT team, who were all inept, incompetent, and unfit for duty. Lucia examined the director's PC and found an empty "Home" folder. "I saw that he had clicked on the 'Up' directory icon to get there," Lucia told On Call. "I casually clicked on the 'Documents' folder and his files magically reappeared." Guess how much gratitude the director showed Lucia. If you guessed zero, congratulations. When Lucia tried to impart a quick lesson in directory navigation, the director was having none of it. "He simply bundled me out of his office, yelling, 'And make sure it doesn't happen again!'" Have your users refused to learn a simple fix? Microsoft annoints Rust as a 'Tier 1' internal language
2026-09-11

Microsoft annoints Rust as a 'Tier 1' internal language

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Redmond's coders get the tool to help them scrub memory bugs off Windows When Microsoft's developers and engineers sit down to code, they can now choose to work in Rust, which Redmond has added to its list of canonical languages. “Rust now is a Tier One language at Microsoft, and that just means that it sits among C++, C# and TypeScript as the best supported languages for internal development in the company,” explained Victor Ciura, Microsoft principal engineer for the Rust tooling team, during a keynote talk today at this year’s annual RustConf, being held this week in Montréal. Ciura said Microsoft has “paved a path” of tools and processes that support local Rust development across the entire software development lifecycle. Rust is no stranger at Microsoft and is already present in over 100 Microsoft project repositories. The company has built Oxidizer, a set of crates to build scalable services in Rust, which have been used to build and refine the Microsoft 365 (M365) core services such as Outlook, Word, Excel, OneDrive, and SharePoint. The Copilot tech stack also owes quite a bit to Rust. But the really messy work still lies ahead. Given the recent Windows Patch Tuesday patch deluge, no doubt one of the largest uses for Rust will be shoring up leaky C/C++ Windows code. This is the work Rust was made for. Mozilla developer Graydon Hoare created Rust as a side project in 2006 chiefly as a high-performance language that, unlike C and C++, guarded against memory bugs. Rewriting programs in Rust to improve performance and minimize bugs has since become a common chore for project leaders, who have Rustified everything from databases to package managers, often with the help of AI. Redmond has already done considerable work making Rust comfy on Microsoft Visual C++ (MSVC), its native platform for compiling Windows binaries. Notably, company engineers built rustc_codegen_utc, a custom Rust compiler code-generation backend that wires the rustc compiler directly into MSVC’s internal toolchain for Windows. “Connecting rustc to that backend lets Rust build on the same platform investment, with perfect compatibility out of the box, rather than requiring a parallel implementation of every Windows-specific capability,” wrote Ciura in a blog post that details Redmond's rusty efforts. “The result is a unified code generation platform for Rust and C++ on Windows.” Even with the tight Rust integration, Microsoft engineers will have their work cut out for them. One of the recurring themes of this year’s RustConf is grappling with the dangerous “unsafe” territory that comes when mixing Rust and C++ code. While the Rust compiler can flag memory errors, it typically doesn’t cover pointers into the C++ memory allocations. Still, Redmond running towards Rust is a welcome development. In his 2025 RustConf keynote, Microsoft Azure CTO Mark Russinovich noted that ~70 percent of Windows CVEs are memory issues. “One of the things that I realized a long time ago is that no matter how much we really want to make C and C++ better, we can't make it as good as what Rust starts with," he said. ® There's no place like the Home directory, but the Documents folder is sometimes nicer Redmond's coders get the tool to help them scrub memory bugs off Windows PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity It’s a better interface, can speed installations, and drives IaaS sales too 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own GPT-Live-1 makes speaking to AI models more fluid War is peace. Oracle says AI will save it from the SaaSpocalypse, not bring it on
2026-09-11

Oracle says AI will save it from the SaaSpocalypse, not bring it on

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
It’s a better interface, can speed installations, and drives IaaS sales too Oracle has argued AI will strengthen its applications business, by giving customers new ways to use software and making them easier to implement. Co-CEO Mike Sicilia decided to defend applications in his first remarks on Oracle’s Q1 FY 2027 earnings call. “The introduction of AI is an accelerator, not a replacement for packaged applications,” he argued. “Before AI came along, application suites had already proven their effectiveness. Companies have been able to increase their profit margins because end-to-end automation of standardized and efficient business processes proved to be much more effective than one-off custom solutions.” The CEO then admitted that business apps “did require organizations to follow workflows and processes as designed in the system, something that many struggle to achieve consistently across functions, teams and regions.” “AI changes this dynamic,” he said. “Rather than asking every employee to navigate and execute a process exactly as a system expects, AI agents can perform tasks using the organization's established workflows and business rules. Employees then shift to overseeing agents, resolving exceptions and applying human judgment where it matters most.” Sicilia is not alone in that view. Salesforce’s Claudeforce puts an AI interface on the CRM giant’s wares. “We are incredibly confident in the potential for this new paradigm to deliver much more rapid ROI for our customers,” Sicilia said. He then promised that in October Oracle will debut an “agentic AI accelerator” that will “automate and orchestrate implementation at an unprecedented scale, compressing SaaS deployments from years to months and months to weeks.” The CEO offered another reason for investors to be bullish about Oracle’s applications biz. “Our SaaS business is also a wonderful lead generation business for our IaaS business,” he said. SaaS is also a solid performer, growing ten percent even as Oracle’s line item for software revenue slipped three percent to $5.5 billion. The database giant’s cloud biz did rather better, growing revenue 60 percent year-over-year to $11.6 billion. Financial analysts on Oracle’s earnings call wanted to know if the company’s colossal investments in datacenters and AI infrastructure are paying off and co-CEO Clay Magouyrk said Oracle is “constantly finding interesting ways to fund the business.” He also pointed to demand for AI infrastructure remaining strong and customers being willing to pay a premium to access it. “GPU longevity and value continue to impress,” he said. “Of all the GPUs that came up for renewal in Q1, that capacity was renewed or resold at a 20 percent premium to prior contracts.” “The majority of those GPUs are four years or older,” he added. “We see a long useful life with increasing value for the AI capacity we're deploying.” That said, Magouyrk warned that some of Oracle’s datacenter mega-builds are progressing faster than others, meaning it can’t offer precise predictions about when its new facilities will start to produce revenue. Magouyrk argued that’s nothing to worry about. “Anyone that's been in the business of doing construction or large-scale infrastructure development, if their plan relies on 100 percent achievement of every one of their deliverables, we have a term for that. It's called a bad plan,” he said. “And so we try real hard not to make bad plans. So we obviously know the difficulty and the complexity of what we're doing, and we don't assume 100 percent of everything is going to work all the time. And we have backup options for those things, as well as we don't count that everything is going to get done exactly on time all the time.” Oracle’s plans did work well enough that it turned on 850 MW of new datacenter capacity in the quarter, and forecasedt earnings per share for the full year would rise five cents to $8.10 on revenue of at least $90 billion. Initial investor response was positive as Oracle shares popped seven percent in after-hours trading, before settling to levels experienced earlier this week. The company’s stock is down 21 percent over the year and is 38 percent below its June peak. ® There's no place like the Home directory, but the Documents folder is sometimes nicer Redmond's coders get the tool to help them scrub memory bugs off Windows PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity It’s a better interface, can speed installations, and drives IaaS sales too 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own GPT-Live-1 makes speaking to AI models more fluid War is peace.
